# batch-03 findings

## WNSZ6xouNv4 — Get Addicted to Improving Your Life - David Goggins
- **Channel / subs / views / likes / true comment count:** Chris Williamson / 4,360,000 / 7,932,606 / 233,554 / 3,239
- **Category:** MEN
- **Thesis (1 sentence):** You are your own purpose, so you must be able to perform with nothing on the calendar — and the fuel for that is pride in yourself, which nobody can give you.
- **Framework:** "performance without a purpose"; "make up your own mission statement" / "ethos in which they want to live by"; two types of "Roger that" — the second being "received orders given expect results" (above and beyond). No sequenced model; loose set of postures.
- **Cause of failure it names:** No pride in self — "you don't care enough about yourself." People "have the ability and just refuse to get off that couch," refuse to "go deeper to go further." Also: dependence on an external event (a race, a school) to generate motion, so when there's no event there's no action.
- **First action prescribed:** Write your own personal mission statement / ethos, the way a company writes one, and hold yourself accountable to it every morning.
- **Time horizon promised:** none stated
- **Emphasis ranking:** 1) identity 2) emotion/motivation 3) goals
- **Measurement:** None. Only "every morning you wake up you hold yourself accountable to that mission." No metric, no log, no review cadence.
- **Prerequisites assumed:** A pre-existing seed of self-respect — he says explicitly that if you don't have it he cannot give it to you. Also physical capacity to train, and enough autonomy over your morning to get up early.
- **Contrarian claims:** Against "find your why": "you need to get to a point in your life where there's nothing on the docket there is no 5K there's no there's no I'm going to get into school to be this or that and still perform to the highest level." Against motivational transferability: "if you have no prior to yourself I can't give it to you." Against sympathy for the struggling: "it's so easy to be great nowadays my friend because most people are weak." Reframes childhood damage as asset: "a lot of the things that you love and value in yourself in adulthood are the light side of something that you were ashamed fearful disgusted by when you were younger."
- **Best lines (3, verbatim, ≤25 words each):**
  1. "it's so easy to be great nowadays my friend because most people are weak"
  2. "if you have no prior to yourself I can't give it to you because you're always going to compromise you're always going to fold"
  3. "so if you wake up in the morning and you don't want to do something you don't care enough about yourself"
- **Audience state from comments:** Addicts and ex-addicts in early sobriety, the obese, the recently unemployed, cancer patients, 70-year-olds, and 17-year-old girls doing their first push-ups. They are not looking for a method — they are looking for permission and a jolt. Many report a concrete streak length ("3 months sober today", "first day running") as a badge.
- **Top comments worth quoting (5, verbatim + like count):**
  1. (10,000) "\"It is so easy to be great now, when so many people are weak\". Strong and true. This guy is a gift, all I can  say."
  2. (6,400) "3 months sober today. Went from a suicidal drug addict and alcoholic and obese to working out twice a day doing 150 Burpees before work and lifting after. Down 30 lbs and going strong 💪"
  3. (4,900) "\"Man....Why am I not doing this for myself?\" \n\nThat shit hit hard. 👊🏽"
  4. (975) "In 2019 I was obese, depressed, had no job and an overall failure. Guys like this inspired me to be better and here I am significantly healthier and just got hired as a firefighter. Anything is possible if you put in the work and want it badly enough"
  5. (693) "Here is how I started waking up early everyday:\n1. Removed my phone from my bedroom: I found my phone distracting before going to bed and after I woke up. I would spend 45 minutes needlessly scrolling.\n2. Placed an alarm outside my bedroom: This forces me to physically get up to go turn it off. By the time I do that I'm already awake and it's hard to go back to sleep.\n3. Immediately Start Purposeful Work: Within 30 minutes of getting up I start working on a purposeful project. I enjoy the work I do so it makes it super easy to get started."
- **Gap the comments expose:** The video supplies affect, not mechanism — and the audience fills the mechanism in themselves. The single most useful comment (693 likes) is a 3-step phone/alarm/first-task protocol that Goggins never mentions. The video also has no answer for the exact person it describes: someone with no pride to start from is told, on camera, that they cannot be helped.
- **Usable for a short?** Yes — "it's so easy to be great nowadays because most people are weak" is a cold-open hook, and "write a mission statement for yourself the way a company writes one" is a stealable 20-second mechanism with an obvious visual.

## Scwsg9Ai3rg — The Myth of Self Improvement
- **Channel / subs / views / likes / true comment count:** Sisyphus 55 / 1,310,000 / 959,935 / 47,929 / 1,540
- **Category:** ANTI
- **Thesis (1 sentence):** Compulsive self-improvement is not the cure for burnout and depression — it is the delivery mechanism, because a culture that tells you what you *can* do extracts more from you than one that tells you what you *must*.
- **Framework:** Borrowed, named verbatim: Foucault's "disciplinary Society" and "Technologies of the self"; Byung-Chul Han's "smart power", "achievement Society", "the auto exploiting subject", "eye tiredness" (I-tiredness); Ehrenberg's "weariness of the self"; Fromm's "escape from freedom" and its cure, "spontaneity"; Tim Kasser's four principles ("self-interest a desire for financial success consumerism and competition"). Diagnostic model, not a how-to.
- **Cause of failure it names:** The goal itself is imported — "we want that which we are supposed to want." Treating the self as a project makes the work infinite by construction, so there is no terminal state and the ego exhausts itself on itself.
- **First action prescribed:** Do something for its own sake — art, play, time with friends, "a gradual slowing down" — and practice listening/observing rather than measuring.
- **Time horizon promised:** none stated
- **Emphasis ranking:** 1) identity 2) environment (culture/economic system) 3) emotion/motivation
- **Measurement:** Explicitly anti-measurement. Tracking is named as the pathology: "we use apps ranking systems and Health Data to determine if we are self-optimizing enough."
- **Prerequisites assumed:** Discretionary time, existing friends to play with, freedom from economic precarity (you must be able to *afford* to stop optimizing), and enough intellectual literacy to follow Foucault/Han/Fromm.
- **Contrarian claims:** "I would argue that it is this cultural emphasis on self-improvement that leaves us so fundamentally burnt out and depressed"; "burnout and depression should be seen more as the very symptoms of this compulsion for achievement"; "in today's America narcissism and sociopathy are strategies and they're successful strategies"; "the sad fact of the matter is that we want that which we are supposed to want"; "negative Freedom the absence of constraints does not bring us closer to solving the question of who we are". Note it hedges at the end: "This is not to suggest that self-improvement or the striving to be a better person is inherently maladaptive."
- **Best lines (3, verbatim, ≤25 words each):**
  1. "the auto exploiting subject carries around its own labor camp"
  2. "the depressed individual is unable to measure up he is tired of having to become himself"
  3. "we exhaust ourselves with ourselves"
- **Audience state from comments:** Self-improvement veterans who are tired of self-improvement and can articulate why. Higher verbal register than any other video in the batch — they argue with the thesis rather than confess to it. A neurodivergent subgroup reports that discipline-based advice is "self pathologizing" for them.
- **Top comments worth quoting (5, verbatim + like count):**
  1. (6,300) "One must imagine Sisyphus self improving"
  2. (4,000) "Great! Now that i know i can plan to be more spontainious."
  3. (2,100) "The problem is not with self improvement, the issue is with the societal values that people are measuring themselves against. As you point out, the grind set is many peoples attempt at self improvement, but those people are optimising themselves to fulfil societies value that more money=better person, it's the nature of capitalism that only a minority of people can achieve this goal, hence the high number of people getting burnt out trying. If we held values like having a strong happy community in equally high regard, then everyone can achieve that and people wouldn't burn out trying to achieve it."
  4. (1,300) "There are two thoughts of self improvement in my opinion: \n\n“I am not my best self. I have been feeding my greatest insecurities and fears. I need to be better, for myself” and “Everyone else is pulling ahead of me in life. I’ve done nothing, I am worthless. I need to improve at something to stay in the running with everyone else”\n\nI think the former is healthy. Making yourself better for yourself is a project you should work on your whole life. The latter is insecurity incarnate and is a standard you’ll never meet. Self improvement is either a lifestyle or an addiction."
  5. (1,100) "\"Why then do we continue to endorse and embrace such a culture?\"\n\n\"This video is sponsored by Brilliant.\""
- **Gap the comments expose:** The prescription is unexecutable and the audience knows it — "Now that i know i can plan to be more spontainious" and "my next step in self improvement should include less self improvement, I'll add it to the list" are both jokes about the same hole. The comments also do the video's missing work: they supply the *discriminator* (intrinsic vs. status-driven motive) that would let a viewer tell healthy striving from auto-exploitation. The video says the distinction matters and never operationalizes it.
- **Usable for a short?** Yes, and it's the best framing device in the batch: run the critique, then hard-cut to the sponsor read — the audience already found that joke, so it lands. "You carry around your own labor camp" is a strong single-line hook.

## mMMerxh_12U — How To Meditate For Beginners - A Definitive Guide
- **Channel / subs / views / likes / true comment count:** Improvement Pill / 3,770,000 / 6,026,323 / 209,059 / 6,257
- **Category:** GEN
- **Thesis (1 sentence):** Breathing meditation is mechanically trivial — the whole difficulty is converting it into a daily habit.
- **Framework:** No named model; a fixed question sequence used as structure: "where should I meditate / what do I do with my body / how long should I meditate for / what to do during meditation / how often should I meditate / when will I start seeing the benefits." Technique named: "mindfulness breathing meditation." Core metaphor: "your mindfulness muscle."
- **Cause of failure it names:** Not technique — habit formation. "meditation is very simple it's just the act of making it into a daily habit that is hard." Secondary: beginners criticize themselves for mind-wandering and quit.
- **First action prescribed:** Set an alarm for 5 minutes, sit on the floor cross-legged with a straight back and level head, eyes closed, and focus on the breath at the nostrils.
- **Time horizon promised:** "it took me about a month of meditating every single day before I noticed any benefit myself"
- **Emphasis ranking:** 1) habits/systems 2) skills 3) emotion/motivation
- **Measurement:** Timer length (5 min → 10–20 min) and daily streak; subjective proxy is how long you can hold attention on the breath before drifting. No log or journal prescribed.
- **Prerequisites assumed:** 5–20 minutes of uninterrupted private time, a floor you can sit on, ability to sit upright, and enough baseline stability that "observe without judging" is achievable.
- **Contrarian claims:** Mild only. Against purists: "a lot of well seasoned meditators will preach about how your feet need to be on top of each other however for the majority of people who are beginners it's perfectly fine to have them crisscrossed." Against method-shopping: breathing meditation "is considered to be just as powerful and insightful as any other form of meditation." Against comfort: "it's best to avoid meditating in bed because you tend to fall asleep."
- **Best lines (3, verbatim, ≤25 words each):**
  1. "meditation is very simple it's just the act of making it into a daily habit that is hard"
  2. "think of this as your mind fulness muscle getting stronger and stronger"
  3. "do not judge it do not criticize it simply observe"
- **Audience state from comments:** Overwhelmingly the depressed, the acutely anxious, students who "became so lazy due to 2020", and self-identified ADHD viewers. They arrive treating meditation as a last-resort mental-health intervention, and they cope with humor — the top comments are jokes about being unable to focus, itching, mosquitoes, and reading comments instead of watching.
- **Top comments worth quoting (5, verbatim + like count):**
  1. (14,000) "Who's here because they're depressed and trying to focus on being positive?"
  2. (6,400) "“Focus on the breath”\nADHD: “you lost me at “focus””"
  3. (3,600) "Me: Wants to learn to meditate to help focus and sleep.\nAlso me: 20 seconds into the video and already distracted by comments."
  4. (1,900) "I've only considered mediation because I've been going through severe anxiety and I've heard it can be helpful wish me luck."
  5. (1,200) "\"Meditation is very simple. It's just the act of making it into a daily habit that is hard.\"   That makes so much sense to me."
- **Gap the comments expose:** The audience is here for depression, anxiety and ADHD, and the video addresses none of them — it treats the viewer as a neutral novice acquiring a posture. It also names the hard part ("making it a daily habit") in the last 30 seconds and then supplies zero habit machinery: no cue, no anchor, no streak system, no relapse plan. And "sit on the floor for 10 minutes undisturbed" is contradicted by commenters who say the blocker is siblings, allergies, and a room they can't be alone in.
- **Usable for a short?** Yes — the 14k-like comment "Who's here because they're depressed" is a better hook than anything in the video itself; pair it with the "mindfulness muscle" rep metaphor for the mechanism.

## -moW9jvvMr4 — A Simple Way to Break a Bad Habit | Judson Brewer | TED
- **Channel / subs / views / likes / true comment count:** TED / 27,700,000 / 16,362,837 / 324,359 / 5,620
- **Category:** SCI
- **Thesis (1 sentence):** You break a habit not by overriding it with willpower but by paying such close attention to the reward that you become viscerally disenchanted with it and stop wanting it.
- **Framework:** The habit loop, named verbatim: "Trigger, behavior, reward" (illustrated as "See food, eat food, feel good, repeat" and "See cool, smoke to be cool, feel good. Repeat."). Failure mode named: "cognitive control." Mechanism named: "disenchantment," moving "from knowledge to wisdom," "knowing it in her bones." Replacement loop: "notice the urge, get curious, feel the joy of letting go and repeat." Neural claim: "default mode network," "posterior cingulate cortex."
- **Cause of failure it names:** Cognitive control is structurally unreliable — the prefrontal cortex "is also the first part of our brain that goes offline when we get stressed out," which is precisely when the urge fires. Intellectual knowledge that a habit is bad does nothing; only bodily knowledge does.
- **First action prescribed:** The next time an urge arises — to smoke, stress-eat, check email, text while driving — do the behavior if you must, but be "curiously aware of what's happening in your body and mind in that moment."
- **Time horizon promised:** None stated; explicitly gradual — "This isn't to say that, poof, magically we quit smoking. But over time..." Context datum: his subjects had already failed "on average, six times."
- **Emphasis ranking:** 1) biology/dopamine 2) habits/systems 3) emotion/motivation
- **Measurement:** None prescribed for the viewer. The only numbers are study-level: "mindfulness training was twice as good as gold standard therapy at helping people quit smoking."
- **Prerequisites assumed:** Almost none — no money, no equipment, no time block, no social access. Requires only interoceptive attention, which is exactly what the ADHD/dissociation-adjacent commenters say they lack.
- **Contrarian claims:** "we dropped the bit about forcing and instead focused on being curious"; "In fact, we even told them to smoke. What? Yeah, we said, 'Go ahead and smoke, just be really curious about what it's like when you do.'"; "we don't have to force ourselves to hold back or restrain ourselves from behavior. We're just less interested in doing it in the first place"; and the reframe of cravings as decomposable — "cravings are simply made up of body sensations."
- **Best lines (3, verbatim, ≤25 words each):**
  1. "See food, eat food, feel good, repeat. Trigger, behavior, reward."
  2. "What she discovered just by being curiously aware when she smoked was that smoking tastes like shit."
  3. "notice the urge, get curious, feel the joy of letting go and repeat."
- **Audience state from comments:** Procrastinators who arrived *while* procrastinating (the top comment, 19k, is exactly this), smokers and ex-smokers, phone compulsives, and people cycling in and out of mental-health treatment who have been told "be mindful" for years without anyone explaining the mechanism. A meaningful minority report actual quits attributed to this talk.
- **Top comments worth quoting (5, verbatim + like count):**
  1. (19,000) "You know you are at rock bottom when you start procatinaning by watching videos about procrastination"
  2. (9,300) "1. Notice the URGE.\n2. Get CURIOUS. \n3. Feel the JOY of letting go. \n4. And, REPEAT.\nAwesome way of giving away bad habits!😊"
  3. (7,300) "I quit smoking 12 years ago. The physical withdraw just lasted the first 48 hours, after that time my body didn't beg me for nicotine anymore. After that all I had to fight was the impulse to light a cigarette. What I learned in the process is that the impulse and crave last no longer than 40 seconds. So next time you want to do what you shouldn't, remember: fight that crave and it will vanish in less than 40 seconds"
  4. (2,100) "So, I googled \"how to change habits\", saw this video, clicked, turns out I watched it 2 years ago, yet to no avail. \nWhat has my life come to.."
  5. (13) "I have been in and out of mental health treatment for many years. Mindfulness is always given as a tool I should use to ground myself. This is the first time it's been explained in a way that makes sense to me, and feels achievable. Thank you.\n\nJust \"focus on your breathing\" or \"think about the sensations in your feet\" doesn't make any sense to me!"
- **Gap the comments expose:** The talk never numbers its own steps — the audience does it for him, repeatedly and near-identically (9,300 / 364 / 316 likes for three separate reconstructions of the same 4-step list). That is a product finding: the mechanism is good enough that people will build the artifact themselves, and there is no artifact. What's missing: a dose (how many urges? how long?), a failure branch (what if curiosity doesn't produce disenchantment because the habit genuinely feels great — see the 1,500-like cake comment), and any re-entry after a lapse. One commenter notes the talk didn't work for them across two years and offers no diagnosis of why.
- **Usable for a short?** Strongest mechanism in the batch. The steal: the "we even told them to smoke" reversal as the hook, then the loop diagram, then the 4-step overlay the audience already wrote for you.

## 515AfqXjSno — The Art Of Getting Women To Chase
- **Channel / subs / views / likes / true comment count:** Coach Corey Wayne / 622,000 / 716,197 / 7,855 / 537
- **Category:** DATE
- **Thesis (1 sentence):** Scarcity creates value — a man should set one date per week and stop initiating entirely once she starts reaching out, because over-pursuit is what kills attraction.
- **Framework:** Named rules rather than steps: "scarcity creates value"; "one date per week"; "measured steps"; the attraction/interest-level scale ("a five six maybe seven as far as her attraction level to you when you first first meet"); "people can hide who they are for the first 90 days of a relationship"; "what's your purpose what's your mission in life"; and the meta-rule, "read the book 10 to 15 times" (*How To Be A 3% Man*). The framework is deliberately located in the paid product, not the video.
- **Cause of failure it names:** Over-pursuing. Every one of the five scenarios is diagnosed as the same thing: "you don't hear those things unless you're pursuing too much." Secondary causes: having no purpose or mission so you're always available; having only one woman in your life; involving mutual friends; cherry-picking video advice instead of reading the book.
- **First action prescribed:** Literally: "well first and foremost you need to read the book 10 to 15 times." Operationally: set one date per week and initiate nothing else.
- **Time horizon promised:** "usually by the second or third date of the second or third week the woman feels safe and comfortable enough to... start reaching out"; frequency builds over "five six seven weeks"; character reveals at 90 days.
- **Emphasis ranking:** 1) social/relationships 2) identity (purpose/mission as the thing that makes you unavailable) 3) skills
- **Measurement:** Inbound contact frequency is the whole dashboard — how often *she* initiates, and whether that rate is rising. Secondary: rating her attraction level 1–10 and watching it move.
- **Prerequisites assumed:** A full life you can't drop (job, gym, hobbies, friends), several women in rotation ("that's why you should have other women in your life"), your own place to bring her back to, a car, and money for weekly dates. Also assumes the $10–$50 book.
- **Contrarian claims:** Eagerness is a red flag, not a green one — "if you meet a girl and you go out on one date and then she calling you every single day and wanting to see you there's a really high likelihood that that girl is incredibly insecure and needy." Rejection is always your fault: "you're never going to hear let's just be friends with a girl who's pursuing you women don't dump guys that they're chasing." Against closure: burn the letter unread. Against group socializing: "no group dates unless she's your girlfriend." Against accommodation, down to the menu: "you're not going to sit there and eat fish when you [ __ ] hate fish."
- **Best lines (3, verbatim, ≤25 words each):**
  1. "when it comes to dating scarcity creates value"
  2. "you can't rush or force a woman to fall in love anymore then you can rush or force a rose to bloom"
  3. "throw a letter in your fireplace and burn it up let it keep you warm for a minute or two"
- **Audience state from comments:** Dumped men and one-itis sufferers, mid-rumination, looking for a rule set that will make an unpredictable outcome legible. Extremely high parasocial trust and low comment volume relative to views (537 on 716k) — this is a devoted, small, returning congregation, not a passing audience. Several report the breakup itself resolved by "getting on purpose."
- **Top comments worth quoting (5, verbatim + like count):**
  1. (956) "Everytime you want to text her, read corey's book as a distraction"
  2. (562) "I think he really wants us to read the book."
  3. (250) "\"well first of all you need to read the book 10-15 times\" I laughed so hard when he started it off with that but it's true."
  4. (201) "The concept of 'purpose' is one of my favorite areas of Coach Corey Wayne's teachings; because I know to be true the effect it has in maintaining a healthy mental and emotional state. My impassioned comments on this topic can be found on many of these great videos, but can be summed up, simply: Follow your purpose, and she'll follow YOU."
  5. (18) "This man changed my life, my ex girlfriend broke up with me little over 2 months ago, I started watching his videos. He talked about a man need to be on his purpose. Of course I begged, pleaded and chased my ex for 2 weeks  before discovering this channel, guys now am on my purpose, have my own business, have my YouTube channel and a lot has changed in my life and guess what? I don’t want my ex anymore."
- **Gap the comments expose:** The advice is circular against its own audience. Its prerequisites — other women in rotation, a busy purposeful life, friends you do things with — are the exact deficits that bring viewers here, and the video treats those deficits as moral failures ("what's your purpose what's your mission in life dude and you need to get some [ __ ] Hobbies") rather than as the thing to build first. Meanwhile the actual how-to is withheld behind the book, and the top comments are all jokes about that withholding. Note the accidental best mechanic in the comments: use the book as a *substitute behavior* for the urge to text her (956 likes) — that's a habit-substitution tactic the video never proposes.
- **Usable for a short?** Yes, narrowly. "Scarcity creates value" as the cold open, and the one-date-per-week → she-initiates → you-stop-initiating handoff is a clean, drawable state machine. The rose line is the emotional closer.

## MruNugpHDV0 — A Simple Way to Organize Your Life
- **Channel / subs / views / likes / true comment count:** Nathaniel Drew / 1,780,000 / 1,896,544 / 70,961 / 1,382
- **Category:** GEN
- **Thesis (1 sentence):** You cannot organize a life you can't see, so get everything out of your head onto paper, prioritize it, and place it on a hand-drawn three-month timeline.
- **Framework:** Three named steps: "Step one fireworks", "step two prioritization", "step three the timeline." Supporting: "the eisenhower matrix", mood boards as a timeline alternative, and his own term "intentional documentation."
- **Cause of failure it names:** An unnamed but explicit skill deficit — "there's a skill that i see people seriously lack it is the ability the capacity to externalize their ideas." Secondary: digital tools put you "two clicks away from a distraction"; and chronic over-estimation of what fits in a span of time.
- **First action prescribed:** Take a physical notepad and pen — not an app — and write/draw everything in your head out, unedited, accepting that it will be chaotic.
- **Time horizon promised:** Three months / 90 days, stated as a hard perceptual limit: "i cannot see clearly beyond three months from now actually i think the magic number is more like two two and a half months."
- **Emphasis ranking:** 1) goals 2) environment (analog paper vs. the distraction surface of the phone) 3) social/relationships (bouncing priorities off trusted people)
- **Measurement:** Weak and self-admittedly loose — "in the span of 90 days you can see measurable progress even if it's just a tiny amount." The only real instrument is redrawing the map repeatedly, each pass adding clarity ("i think i drew it out maybe four times").
- **Prerequisites assumed:** Notepads, unstructured self-directed work (this is a creator/freelancer's planning system — it assumes you choose your own projects), quiet time to draw, and a bench of respected honest friends who "are also willing to tell you the truth and not be yes yes men."
- **Contrarian claims:** Flatly anti-software, from a digital creator: "there isn't a productivity app or piece of software in existence that comes anywhere close to the power of the pen and paper in my opinion." Anti-long-horizon planning: three months is the maximum legible distance. Anti-tidiness at the start: "this first stage is really quite chaotic and that's totally fine... order will come later." De-emphasizes the system itself: "what matters more than the system itself whether you use a matrix like this or not is this kind of thinking."
- **Best lines (3, verbatim, ≤25 words each):**
  1. "there isn't a productivity app or piece of software in existence that comes anywhere close to the power of the pen and paper"
  2. "there's a skill that i see people seriously lack it is the ability the capacity to externalize their ideas"
  3. "whenever you're on your phone or your computer you're always two clicks away from a distraction"
- **Audience state from comments:** Scattered, aesthetically-motivated young people who feel behind and are drawn as much to the craft of the video as the advice. Tone is admiration bordering on parasocial ("I think you are the only person on youtube who i trust for advice on how to get my shit together"). Notably includes older viewers taking advice from a 22-year-old and remarking on it.
- **Top comments worth quoting (5, verbatim + like count):**
  1. (4,800) "\"There isn't a productivity app or piece of software in existence that comes anywhere close to the power of a pen and a paper\" Can't agree more with this! ✏📖"
  2. (2,500) "When a digital creator says that he prefers analog versus digital despite the obvious contradiction you know he says the truth. Thank you for the honesty Nathaniel, mad respect."
  3. (1,900) "“I wish I had a thousand lives. Then I would have the time to do all the things that I want to do and create all the things that I want to create. But I don’t. I have this life, and so the next best thing is to make the most of it.” I feel like this is something that people will be quoting 50 years from now."
  4. (340) "I think you are the only person on youtube who i trust for advice on how to get my shit together"
  5. (228) "So basically it's \n\nS1:  put it down\nS2:  look at it\nS3:  get it done"
- **Gap the comments expose:** There is no execution layer. The 228-like summary — "S1: put it down / S2: look at it / S3: get it done" — is a precise indictment: the video ends at the drawn timeline and says nothing about weekly review, accountability, what to do when the plan drifts, or how the timeline gets consulted again after it's made. It also gives no criteria for prioritizing beyond "bounce it off someone you respect," which quietly requires a social asset most viewers watching a "how do I get my life together" video don't have.
- **Usable for a short?** Yes — "Fireworks" is a memorable name for the brain-dump step, and the hand-drawn 90-day timeline is inherently filmable. Strongest single claim to steal: you cannot see past ~90 days, so stop planning past it.

## IitIl2C3Iy8 — Six behaviors to increase your confidence | Emily Jaenson | TEDxReno
- **Channel / subs / views / likes / true comment count:** TEDx Talks / 44,600,000 / 5,860,914 / 133,596 / 1,580
- **Category:** Manifest says DATE; it is not dating advice for men — it's behavior-first confidence building for women in male-dominated workplaces. Kept in scope because it's a pure "how change actually happens" argument.
- **Thesis (1 sentence):** Confidence is not a prerequisite for acting confidently — you install the behaviors first and the attitude follows.
- **Framework:** Six named behaviors: 1) "count yourself in" 2) "give yourself 20 seconds of courage" 3) "take a seat at the table" 4) "cheer for other people's success" 5) "bolster your confidence for a new activity through your already great performance in another" 6) "celebrate constantly." Preceded by three identity questions: "Who am I?" "Who do I want to become?" "How does this person I want to become behave?"
- **Cause of failure it names:** People try to change the attitude directly. "my behaviors did not align with these confident attitudes" — and waiting for the feeling to arrive first means the behaviors never start.
- **First action prescribed:** Count "three, two, one, go" out loud or in your head and physically start moving toward the thing you're avoiding.
- **Time horizon promised:** Immediate on the behaviors — "six behaviors you can start today" — against a personal arc of "13 years later, I became the first female general manager of a Triple-A baseball team."
- **Emphasis ranking:** 1) identity 2) habits/systems (behaviors as the lever) 3) social/relationships
- **Measurement:** Celebration as deliberate reinforcement, framed neurologically: "This will create a marker in your brain to rewire and reinforce the behaviors that led to success in the first place." Concrete instrument: "creating a highlight reel on your cell phone of your most proud accomplishments." Also step 5's audit — reread your job description and annotate your accolades against each line.
- **Prerequisites assumed:** A professional context with actual tables, meetings and colleagues; a career ladder to climb; prior accomplishments to mine for step 5 (which is circular for anyone starting from zero); enough job security that speaking up is survivable.
- **Contrarian claims:** Behavior precedes attitude, against mindset-first orthodoxy: "Research tells us that in order to get people to change, you need to not start with the attitudes, but with the behaviors associated with those attitudes." Anti-zero-sum: "Confident people celebrate the success of others rather than feeling threatened," with the Amy Poehler line "Good for her, not for me." And a deliberately literal reading of a cliché: "Not metaphorically speaking; actually, take a seat at the table."
- **Best lines (3, verbatim, ≤25 words each):**
  1. "Take a seat at the table. Not metaphorically speaking; actually, take a seat at the table."
  2. "Confidence is born in all we've already done and already achieved."
  3. "how many runs could you score if you were 10 times more confident?"
- **Audience state from comments:** Women who freeze specifically when asking for something — "I am very confident at speaking to strangers on general topics. But, when it comes seeking help or asking for an opportunity I act like mute." Also English learners using it as listening practice, and a heavy layer of astroturfed self-help book spam ("Woman He Can't Unsee", "Why Everybody Loves Me") targeting exactly this insecurity.
- **Top comments worth quoting (5, verbatim + like count):**
  1. (11,000) "“When nobody else celebrates you, learn to celebrate yourself. When nobody else compliments you, then compliment yourself. It’s not up to other people to keep you encouraged. It’s up to you. Encouragement should come from the inside.” – Jay Shetty ✨"
  2. (3,500) "Taking risks and going out of  comfort zone is the key to self-confidence"
  3. (943) "I attest of number 5.I wrote all my accomplishments before a job interview. I really wanted that role, so was already confident when I got to the interview. Didn’t just secure the role, they gave me a role that is higher than what I applied. Remember your accomplishments, remember your testimonies. They will keep you grateful and going😊"
  4. (81) "The way she delivered the speech proves that what she said really works."
  5. (14) "I am very confident at speaking to strangers on general topics. But, when it comes seeking help or asking for an opportunity I act like mute. Suddenly my super introverted nature kicks in. But, now I know how to act confidently at work. Thanks Emily ❤"
- **Gap the comments expose:** The two highest-engagement comments are a timestamped re-list and a second full re-list of the six steps — the audience wants the checklist, not the talk, and nobody shipped it as an artifact. Substantively: there is no failure branch. Nothing covers the 20 seconds of courage going badly, being talked over after you take the seat, or how to run step 5 when you have no prior wins to draw on. And the whole thing presumes a workplace; there is nothing here for someone whose problem is that there is no table.
- **Usable for a short?** Very high. "20 seconds of courage" and the 3-2-1 countdown are pre-packaged short-form mechanics with built-in on-screen visuals, and the pizza-phone-call opener → GM of a baseball team is a complete before/after arc in two sentences.

## yJc4Fst1LMg — How To Approach ANY GIRL in Public (Without Being Creepy)
- **Channel / subs / views / likes / true comment count:** Coach Kyle / 575,000 / 2,261,814 / 46,865 / 2,059
- **Category:** DATE
- **Thesis (1 sentence):** Cold approach fails on mechanics, not courage — most rejections are triggered by your angle, face and voice before you've said anything worth judging.
- **Framework:** "the five key elements": 1) "looks" 2) "the distance and angle of your approach" 3) "your smile" 4) "your voice" 5) "what you say." Supporting concepts: "the open actually starts the moment the girl sees you"; threat-assessment framing ("is he a threat or potential person of value"); the tonality target of "slightly authoritative" with "downward pitch tonality"; and the direct/indirect midpoint for the opener.
- **Cause of failure it names:** You trigger her instinctual fight-or-flight before the interaction starts — approaching from behind, closing distance too fast, jumping in front of her, touching her, no smile ("a very serious serial killer type look on my face"), and a "weak and apologetic" voice she may not even register as being addressed to her. Then either being too direct (pressure) or too indirect (pointless).
- **First action prescribed:** Smile before she sees you, then move in from the side at a safe distance while staying slightly in front of her.
- **Time horizon promised:** none stated
- **Emphasis ranking:** 1) skills 2) biology/dopamine (instinctive threat categorization) 3) emotion/motivation
- **Measurement:** None. The only quantity in the whole video is his own baseline anecdote — "back in 2014 when I did my first five approaches with women they all resulted in an automatic rejection." No approach count, no target, no log.
- **Prerequisites assumed:** Acceptable "fashion your physique your grooming your face your presentation as a whole" — stated bluntly as a gate. Also: a walkable daytime public environment, physical ability to keep pace walking alongside her, and free daytime hours.
- **Contrarian claims:** Against the confidence-is-everything consensus: "looks and yes looks do matter guys." Against the stop-her-at-all-costs school: "be prepared that you may have to keep walking with her at least until we see that you're cool." Against reading her first: smile *before* you look at her. And the direct reframe of politeness as a defect — apologizing for approaching reads as insecurity, not manners.
- **Best lines (3, verbatim, ≤25 words each):**
  1. "the open actually starts the moment the girl sees you"
  2. "approaching from behind is creepy and touching us is just worse so just keep your hands to yourself"
  3. "I had a very serious serial killer type look on my face"
- **Audience state from comments:** Approach-anxious men, plus a large share of drive-by and clearly astroturfed comments pushing fake books ("Flirtosphere Seduction", "Celestial Soulmate", "The Shadow Game of Attraction") — the comment section is heavily farmed, which is itself a signal about how monetizable this exact pain is. Genuine comments skew toward tactical additions and gallows humor; one disabled viewer notes he'll never use it.
- **Top comments worth quoting (5, verbatim + like count):**
  1. (1,700) "Coming from watching tons of cold approach videos, most of the points are legit. But one thing I would add is prepare to be rejected and have an escape route. “Excuse, I just have to say I like your style” if she just says thanks and continues to walk away, be prepared to say “ have a good day” and walk away with confidence. It’s important to always keep that confidence in check"
  2. (576) "Why does this feel like a combat tutorial?"
  3. (274) "I have friends who do randomly do funny stuff like walk right into a girl to talk to them or say something out of the blue and most of the times it works. I think it’s the best way to get rid of that awkward anxiety when you do approach a girl and just be funny about it"
  4. (20) "If you're going to a very pretty women, she most definitely knows it, guys always tell her that. \nMy advice: your impact on a girl will be far more powerful when she has to make the conclusion herself. Instead of opening her with a direct compliment (which in most of the case will get a \"thank you\" point blank). Go for saying something that suggest she's pretty or that she stands out. \nTo me opening a girl and saying \"I just saw you and I felt I had to say hi/meet you\" is far better than \"Hi I find you pretty,glowing whatever\""
  5. (5) "I have a spinal cord lesion I got in my twenties (I live in a third world country, can't do much activity without getting a lot of pain, so very hard to do a lot of normal jobs, and no health care since I need to work for a business or pay for it myself). So I doubt I'll ever use this. But still pretty interesting. The production quality is really good too."
- **Gap the comments expose:** The video opens by naming approach anxiety — "the anxiety kicks in your brain starts telling you all these reasons to not go up" — and then never returns to it; the remaining six minutes are body mechanics. It also stops at the opener: no rejection handling, no exit line, no what-happens-next. The 1,700-like comment supplies precisely the missing piece (an escape route and a graceful exit script), and the 576-like "Why does this feel like a combat tutorial?" names the tonal cost of treating a social act as a threat-mitigation procedure.
- **Usable for a short?** Yes — the approach-angle geometry (behind = creepy, head-on = flight response, side-and-slightly-ahead = safe) is the most genuinely diagrammable mechanism in the batch, and "the open starts the moment she sees you" is a clean hook.
