# batch-11 findings

## 7e6RSGdvHD8 — Give me 15 Minutes and I'll Make you Dangerously Confident
- **Channel / subs / views / likes / true comment count:** Leila Hormozi / 1,680,000 / 8,651,014 / 385,891 / 4,848
- **Category:** DATE (mislabeled in manifest — actually GEN/business-confidence, no dating content)
- **Thesis (1 sentence):** Confidence is an output of accumulated action-evidence, not an input you generate with belief.
- **Framework:** Unnamed 4-part sequence, verbatim beats: (1) "confidence is the output, not the input" / "emotion follows motion"; (2) "a habit that tracks the truth, not your mood" — daily tracking; (3) "tiny tweaks" — "start with something small, so small that you can't lose"; (4) "Choosing to tolerate fear and discomfort rather than trying to eliminate it." Visual metaphor: put the fear "all in my purse" and take it with you.
- **Cause of failure it names:** People chase the result and try to feel ready first — affirmations, manifesting, waiting for fear to go — and they "take too big of a leap," fail, then conclude they're "the kind of person who doesn't achieve their goals."
- **First action prescribed:** Pick something so small you can't lose and start logging it daily — her worked example is a coach whose clients spend week one only being asked "to make their bed and take a picture."
- **Time horizon promised:** None stated for the outcome; internal markers only — "terrified for the first 30 days," 7-day starter habit, quarterly goal cycles broken into ~20 steps.
- **Emphasis ranking:** 1. identity (evidence → identity) · 2. emotion/motivation (fear tolerance) · 3. habits/systems (tracking, tiny steps)
- **Measurement:** Central. Daily tracking of food/workouts/wins; break each quarterly goal into ~20 steps and "hit check, check, check" daily; read progress Monday-to-Monday, not day-to-day, because "progress doesn't go like this."
- **Prerequisites assumed:** Time to train daily reps, a job or arena to be scared in, a team/manager who will hand you a 60-second speaking slot; her own story assumes $5,000 savings and ability to relocate. No money assumed of the viewer.
- **Contrarian claims:** "I had it written on my mirror... And guess what? Nothing changed. It didn't do anything." · "confidence comes from data, not dopamine" · "our brains are wired for survival, not success" · "it is crazy to think that you would actually have confidence when you haven't done the thing yet" · "instead of trying to meditate or trying to affirm or trying to manifest the fear away, you are instead going to say, you know what, I'm going to take the fear."
- **Best lines (3, verbatim, ≤25 words each):**
  - "Fear is a mile wide and an inch deep."
  - "And that's when I realized confidence comes from data, not dopamine."
  - "I'm not confident because I don't have fear. I'm confident because I do things despite the fear."
- **Audience state from comments:** Mixed-gender mass audience; heavy summarizer/list-taker behavior (two of the top comments are 20- and 5-point recaps). Real pain underneath: bereavement ("My husband died last year"), lifelong social anxiety, procrastination. Also visibly infested with book-spam bot comments (Landon Sorel, Luna Rivers).
- **Top comments worth quoting (5, verbatim + like count):**
  - "“Confidence comes from data, not dopamine” this line is to hard" — 7,600
  - "Once you've overcome your fear, you're on a high. You then start chasing your fears instead of your fear chasing you." — 6,200
  - "Confidence isn’t walking into a room thinking you’re better than everyone. It’s walking in knowing you don’t need to compare yourself to anyone." — 5,400
  - "Huge! Thank you. My husband died last year. 1rst year numb, going into second year it's real and harder than the first." — 3,800
  - "This made me cry. A lifetime of severe social anxiety and procrastination with no help. Just thank you" — 446
- **Gap the comments expose:** Nobody asks *what* to track or *how* to know a rep counted — the top comments convert the video back into a 20-line affirmation list, i.e. exactly the thing she says doesn't work. No commenter reports a tracking system; they report a feeling. Also: no help for people whose blocker is grief or clinical anxiety, whom she addresses with the same "do it scared."
- **Usable for a short?** Yes — "confidence comes from data, not dopamine" is the hook, and the purse-full-of-fear image plus a jagged-vs-straight progress line is a ready-made 3-minute visual.

## X3vRK2P9lSU — How to Be More DISCIPLINED - 6 Ways to Master Self Control
- **Channel / subs / views / likes / true comment count:** Thomas Frank / 3,010,000 / 3,306,870 / 114,879 / 1,890
- **Category:** MEN (in practice gender-neutral student productivity)
- **Thesis (1 sentence):** Discipline is a separate, trainable capacity from motivation — "your ability to do it regardless of how you feel" — and it is built by six specific practices, identity change first.
- **Framework:** Explicitly nests discipline inside four areas of "behavior change": self-discipline, habits, environment customization, (joke: Brawndo). Then six named strategies, per chapter titles: "Change in Identity" · "Why" (Jim Carrey's $10M check) · "Embrace" discomfort · "Target the Fundamentals" (sleep, nutrition, exercise) · "Practice Meditation" (zazen) · "Practice Building New Habits."
- **Cause of failure it names:** People conflate discipline with motivation and wait to feel like it; and they try to change behavior while chasing outcome goals rather than an identity, so they keep "carrying around the same beliefs."
- **First action prescribed:** Reframe the change as an identity, not a goal — literally adopt the sentence form "No thanks, I'm not a smoker," and start thinking of yourself as the noun (he chose "athlete").
- **Time horizon promised:** None stated; his own identity shift is described as working "in the past couple of months."
- **Emphasis ranking:** 1. identity · 2. habits/systems · 3. biology/dopamine (sleep/nutrition/exercise as executive-function fuel)
- **Measurement:** Habit tracking apps named — "Habitica, or Strides, which is my current favorite one, or HabitBull on Android" — with the explicit rule to watch trend not streak: "not focus too much on purity, on perfect streaks."
- **Prerequisites assumed:** Baseline health controllable (sleep, food, gym), student-ish free time, money for apps/Brilliant subscription, no acute mental illness. Assumes a learnable-skill outlet ("an independent learning project").
- **Contrarian claims:** "forget about the goal you're trying to achieve, the external outcome, and instead focus on the change in identity you want to happen" · "I am not a huge fan of meditation, it's probably the self-development practice that I hate doing the most, but the benefits of doing so are undeniable" · "if you're sleep deprived... or your diet is crap, that is where you should focus your discipline first" (i.e. don't start with the goal at all).
- **Best lines (3, verbatim, ≤25 words each):**
  - "Self-discipline is about leaning into resistance, taking action in spite of how you feel, living life by design, not by default."
  - "motivation is your overall level of desire to do something, whereas discipline is your ability to do it regardless of how you feel."
  - "Always remember that you are first and foremost a collection of biological systems, all of which require the right inputs"
- **Audience state from comments:** Students and procrastinators caught mid-avoidance ("Why does it feel like he caught me procrastinating and uploaded a video to stop me"), mood-dependent workers, people who burn out within days of white-knuckling. Same recap-listing behavior; same book-spam.
- **Top comments worth quoting (5, verbatim + like count):**
  - "“I think everybody should get rich and famous and do everything they ever dreamed of so they can see that it's not the answer.” - Jim Carey" — 3,600
  - "Cold showers are honestly a gateway, highly recommend." — 3,100
  - "I have a bad habit of studying according to my mood 🙃 I will sit one day for hours studying and then stop for the next three days💆‍♀️.  I want to do the best everyday😔" — 1,100
  - "Why does it feel like he caught me procrastinating and uploaded a video to stop me from doing so" — 632
  - "\"Start thinking in behavior change in terms of The person you want to embody, rather than the goals that you want to achieve.\"\nThat phrase hit me like a truck, thank you, Tom." — 564
- **Gap the comments expose:** The mechanics of sequencing. A 2,600-like comment asks outright for the prerequisite he skipped: "can you please make a video on How to go to bed on time?.. I've been trying to build the habit of waking up early and I've failed to reach automaticity because I struggle to go to bed on time." Nobody is given a way to *pick* which identity or which first habit; they're given six parallel strategies and left to choose.
- **Usable for a short?** Yes — the two-smokers script ("No thanks, I'm trying to quit" vs "No thanks, I'm not a smoker") is a clean two-shot dramatization; pair with the cold-shower "rep" metaphor.

## HWAt8j9WLB0 — How To Change Your Life In 50 Days | Project 50 Challenge 2025
- **Channel / subs / views / likes / true comment count:** Tenzin Lhoden / 22,200 / 1,495,847 / 67,635 / 1,327
- **Category:** Manifest says ANTI — wrong. This is a pure GEN/MEN challenge-compliance video with zero critique of self-help.
- **Thesis (1 sentence):** Running seven fixed daily rules for 50 straight days, restarting from day one on any miss, is what changes you mentally and physically.
- **Framework:** "the project 50 challenge," created by "state of mind," seven rules: (1) "wake up before 8 am" (2) "read 10 pages a day" non-fiction (3) "exercise before one hour a day" (4) "no junk food and alcohol" (5) "dedicate one hour towards a new skill" (6) "do your morning routine" (7) "track your daily progress in a journal." Plus the reset rule: "if you miss one rule you have to start all over again from day one."
- **Cause of failure it names:** Not addressed as psychology — failure is defined purely mechanically as breaking a rule (social occasions are the named threat: late nights with friends, friends ordering burgers, friends drinking beer). Implicit cause: inconsistency, "we don't grow when things are easy."
- **First action prescribed:** Wake up before 8 am tomorrow and begin day one of all seven rules simultaneously.
- **Time horizon promised:** 50 days.
- **Emphasis ranking:** 1. habits/systems · 2. biology/dopamine (sleep, food, exercise, no alcohol) · 3. skills (1 hr/day new skill + 10 pages)
- **Measurement:** Rule 7 is the measurement: daily journal of "how my day was how i was feeling my thoughts and my goals for the day"; he substitutes private video vlogs. Plus public documentation on Instagram/TikTok "they will give you some kind of accountability."
- **Prerequisites assumed:** Large discretionary time (2+ hours/day of exercise and skill work), gym or home-workout space, money for good food and books, control over your own sleep schedule, no shift work, no caregiving. Assumes friends whose social life you can decline.
- **Contrarian claims:** Only one, and it undercuts the challenge he's promoting: "i don't think it's good to work out every day especially weight lifting because your muscle need time to rest." Otherwise it's orthodox.
- **Best lines (3, verbatim, ≤25 words each):**
  - "we don't grow when things are easy we grow when we face challenges"
  - "the hard part is if you miss one rule you have to start all over again from day one"
  - "it's more important to be consistently good rather than occasionally great"
- **Audience state from comments:** Very young, pre-commitment, and using the comment box as a public start-line. Multiple people date-stamp a promise to return; a 14-year-old; ADHD self-snackers; self-described "lazy, dull, unhealthy, inconsistent and aimless." Almost nobody comes back to report a finish.
- **Top comments worth quoting (5, verbatim + like count):**
  - "1- Wake up before 8\n2-read 10 pages a day\n3-exercise 1 hr\n4- no junk food\n5-dedicate 1 hour for learning new skill\n6- do morning routine\n7- track daily progress, write or talk about your day\nBe consistent if u stop doing it even a single day then u have to start again from 1" — 2,500
  - "No junk food is gonna be the hardest. I have ADHD and I don’t drink or smoke at all, so sometimes I snack or binge eat or have a nice energy drink or soda to get me flowing and focused again." — 35
  - "Plz like this comment so that i can come here after 50 days it's a challenge to myself" — 8
  - "In my life i have been  lazy, dull, unhealthy , inconsistentand aimless. but i want to  give me another  chance . I want a comeback in my life" — 5
  - "This one video >>>>> Other self development videos \nIam not going to watch another self development video, this video is enough ❤❤" — 2
- **Gap the comments expose:** Nothing exists for the miss. The restart rule is the whole difficulty and the video gives no recovery protocol, no scaling for ADHD/shift work, and no answer to "what happens on day 51." Commenters conspicuously post day-one pledges and never post completions — the artifact of a design that makes day 34 worth the same as day 0.
- **Usable for a short?** Yes, as the *counter-example*: "miss one rule, restart from day one" is a visceral hook, and the comment section full of day-one pledges with no finishers is the visual proof that all-or-nothing design fails.

## M6NsEDwHHiE — The drawing advice that changed my life
- **Channel / subs / views / likes / true comment count:** struthless / 1,220,000 / 3,690,460 / 289,799 / 8,962
- **Category:** GEN
- **Thesis (1 sentence):** Doing one identical small thing every single day beats scattering effort, because starting — not deliberating — is what reveals the thing you're actually looking for.
- **Framework:** One instruction — "draw the same thing every single day... just try it for a year and then we'll reassess" — justified by four named reasons, per chapter titles: "quantity leads to quality" · "it promotes constraints for creativity" · "action comes before motivation" · "it removes \"thinking\" as a form of procrastination" (donkey parable).
- **Cause of failure it names:** Scatter plus deliberation-as-productivity. "one day you write a song the next day you write a poem and then the third day you do a drawing and none of it adds up to anything"; and "I was using thinking as a form of procrastination."
- **First action prescribed:** Pick one subject — have someone else pick it if you can't — and draw it today; he drew nine ibises the first night and posted them.
- **Time horizon promised:** One year, explicitly, with a review point at the end ("then we'll reassess"). Boredom arrives fast — he says he got bored of drawing ibises within weeks and the pivot to social commentary came around "the hundredth."
- **Emphasis ranking:** 1. habits/systems (daily identical rep, constraint) · 2. emotion/motivation (action precedes motivation) · 3. identity (from "scattered creative" to person who ships)
- **Measurement:** None formal — the count of drawings is the only metric, and the visible Instagram archive is the accountability. Explicitly anti-perfection: "the reason that my videos are now gonna be 70% instead of 100% perfect."
- **Prerequisites assumed:** A mentor figure willing to give tough love, a creative medium already available, ~30 min/day, a public posting account. Assumes you have too many interests, not none.
- **Contrarian claims:** "quantity leads to quality" (against craft-first orthodoxy) · "action comes before motivation it's not the other way around... firstly you need to act and only then will you find your motivation" · "thinking about stuff is not doing stuff it's the complete opposite of doing stuff" · against creative freedom: "immediately having a constraint lets you know exactly what you're going to be doing" · "he wasn't telling me to draw an iris he was just telling me to start" (auto-caption mishears "ibis").
- **Best lines (3, verbatim, ≤25 words each):**
  - "all you're doing is laying a single brick of a million different houses and expecting that one day it'll magically become a mansion"
  - "thinking about stuff is not doing stuff it's the complete opposite of doing stuff"
  - "I was the donkey I was dumb I was like should I make this should I make that"
- **Audience state from comments:** Creatives paralysed by option-overload and ADHD-style project-hopping, who came for drawing tips and got diagnosed. Highest engagement in the batch relative to views. Notably contains actual outcome reports, which almost no other video in this batch has.
- **Top comments worth quoting (5, verbatim + like count):**
  - "\"Thinking about stuff is not doing stuff\" hit me like a brick" — 25,000
  - "When you came here for lil drawing advice and heck now it feels like Ive figured everything in my life." — 8,800
  - "\"Action comes before motivation\" - I need to glue this to my freakin wall." — 4,900
  - "There was this quote I once saw that fits here really well:\n\"If you commit to nothing, you will be distracted by everything.\"" — 4,500
  - "I'm on drawing day 359 thanks to your advice.   Just checking back to let you know I just moved into my very own studio space.  Your advice has changed everything for me." — 235
- **Gap the comments expose:** Nobody is told how to choose the one thing, and the comments show them stalling on exactly that (people announcing "My one drawing a day is going to be a cat, I think"). His own choice was made *for* him by a mentor — the mechanism that unlocked him is the one thing a viewer can't get from the video. Also unaddressed: what to do when the daily rep gets boring, which he admits happened to him and solved only by accident.
- **Usable for a short?** Strongest in the batch — "one brick of a million different houses," the donkey that dies between hay and water, and the visible ibis-archive escalation are three stealable visuals with one thesis.

## 2bQjfTHlwzU — Approaching Girls made Easy! (For Introverts & Beginners)
- **Channel / subs / views / likes / true comment count:** Todd V Dating / 311,000 / 372,559 / 13,896 / 633
- **Category:** DATE
- **Thesis (1 sentence):** Approach anxiety is beaten by graduated desensitization — a five-step ladder where each rung is barely harder than the last and every rung ends with permission to walk away.
- **Framework:** Five layered steps, verbatim: (1) "leave the house" (2) "physically walk over say literally anything and then you're free to go" (3) add a compliment (4) "introducing ourselves" (5) "introduce one new topic to the conversation," carrying it "for another 10 to 30 seconds." Governing rule: "do each step until it seems easy and then move on to the next one." Named mechanism: "desensitization."
- **Cause of failure it names:** Men never reach step one — "they sit around in their living rooms thinking about doing this" — and those who do jump straight to the hard version: "it's better to start out too easy and let the process be gradual than it is to dive right in make it scary and then quit on yourself." Secondary cause: attaching self-worth to her reaction.
- **First action prescribed:** "get out of your house go somewhere where there are actual women."
- **Time horizon promised:** None stated — the ladder is self-paced by an ease criterion, not a calendar.
- **Emphasis ranking:** 1. skills (a drilled behavioural ladder) · 2. emotion/motivation (fear tolerance, detaching from outcome) · 3. environment (leaving the house, being where women are)
- **Measurement:** No count, no journal — the only gauge is subjective: repeat a step until "it seems easy." Redefines the scoreboard: "as long as you're doing something you're winning if you're sitting around doing nothing you are not winning."
- **Prerequisites assumed:** Physical mobility and a walkable place with strangers; free time; no assumption of looks, money, or existing friends — the least prerequisite-heavy video in the batch.
- **Contrarian claims:** "this is not about you and the girl just yet this is about you and yourself it's about you versus you" · "it does not have to go well you do not have to have an ego about it you don't have to attach your self-worth to it" · "approaching is primarily a physical act" · anti-technique: "you could technically just walk up and introduce yourself but that tends to be a really bad approach and i don't recommend it" · explicitly permits abandoning the interaction at every rung.
- **Best lines (3, verbatim, ≤25 words each):**
  - "if you're most guys the actual answer is nothing they would do nothing and then they'd sit there stewing about it"
  - "approaching is primarily a physical act it's the act of putting one foot in front of another"
  - "it's not about what she gives you it's about what you do for yourself"
- **Audience state from comments:** Severe, self-described floor-level cases — crippling approach anxiety, social isolation, depression, a 55-year-old alcoholic in recovery with no relationship history. Also the only comment section in the batch with concrete behaviour-change reports ("today i got rejected and its better than not approaching").
- **Top comments worth quoting (5, verbatim + like count):**
  - "Remember that confidence is not the lack of insecurity; its the willingness to act in spite of insecurity." — 1,500
  - "i pushed myself to cold approach because of this video, today i got rejected and its better than not approaching and regretting it later :D thanks" — 699
  - "im an introvert . i once approached a girl and told her i like her shirt. that girl is now my wife and we are expecting a baby soon . its that simple ..." — 402
  - "All the programs and coaches miss this very important step of getting from lvl -5 to lv1. For people like me with crippling social and aproach enxiety, with no clue or experience in social situations, with depression and crippling self doubt, this is the way to start." — 102
  - "Somehow I never learned this basic skill and need someone to break it down step-by-step for me like I'm five. Something's wrong in society when men aren't learning this naturally." — 20
- **Gap the comments expose:** The ladder ends exactly where the hard part starts. Commenters supply the missing rungs themselves — one top comment invents a pre-step (talk to waitresses and clerks with non-transactional questions), and the "lvl -5 to lvl 1" comment names the gap explicitly: every other program starts above where they are, and this one stops before the conversation actually has to go anywhere. No rep count, no schedule, no way to know you've plateaued.
- **Usable for a short?** Yes — "you're free to go the moment after" is the strongest single mechanism in the batch: a permission-to-leave clause that makes the scary action cost nothing. Ladder graphic, five rungs, exit door on each.

## yifQZm602IY — How To Be Instantly More Fun to Talk To
- **Channel / subs / views / likes / true comment count:** Charisma on Command / 7,090,000 / 6,102,151 / 190,604 / 4,775
- **Category:** DATE (functionally a general social-skills video, not dating-specific)
- **Thesis (1 sentence):** Being boring is five specific, correctable conversational behaviours, not a personality.
- **Framework:** Five named mistakes with a fix each: "energy ducking" → be first to initiate playfulness, answer "with an absurd non-literal answer"; "assuming interest" → open with a "story gap"; "giving bland one to five word answers" → elaborate then leave space; "ask the same default boring questions" → ask what they're excited to answer, or "fun hypotheticals"; "being a passive listener" → "mirroring" and "listening to laugh." Every fix is demonstrated with celebrity clip evidence (Jack Black, Chris Pratt, Kevin Hart, Sean Evans, Joe Rogan, Craig Ferguson).
- **Cause of failure it names:** Optimising for not standing out. "energy ducking is when you come into a conversation with low enthusiasm to avoid standing out... you avoid making a negative or positive impression." Plus a bad model of charisma — thinking you must be impressive rather than make the other person feel impressive.
- **First action prescribed:** Next time someone asks you a question, answer it with an absurd non-literal answer before giving the real one.
- **Time horizon promised:** "Instantly" in the title; the upsell course is "20 to 30 minutes a day" and a member testimonial claims "life changing in six weeks."
- **Emphasis ranking:** 1. skills · 2. social/relationships · 3. emotion/motivation (state priming before going out)
- **Measurement:** None — no tracking, no reps, no journal. The only feedback loop offered is watching the other person's reaction in the moment (the guest "smiles wider and wider").
- **Prerequisites assumed:** A supply of conversations to practise on; enough baseline social access that the problem is quality not opportunity; ability to observe and imitate; money for the course if you want the sequencing.
- **Contrarian claims:** "sometimes the thing that most makes someone like you is not when you are smart or funny but when you make them feel smart or funny" · "having a memorable conversation doesn't mean you have to do all the talking in fact great conversationalists do the opposite" · against authenticity-first advice, it prescribes deliberate priming: "a good practice is to watch something you know will make you laugh right before you go out."
- **Best lines (3, verbatim, ≤25 words each):**
  - "energy ducking is when you come into a conversation with low enthusiasm to avoid standing out"
  - "a story gap is when you build interest in a story by hinting at how it ends without spoiling the punchline"
  - "most people want to have fun funny conversations they just aren't sure how"
- **Audience state from comments:** Self-identified introverts, autistic and socially anxious viewers studying humans as if from outside — and a large ironic contingent who find the whole thing exhausting or alienating. Much darker and more resigned than the video's tone.
- **Top comments worth quoting (5, verbatim + like count):**
  - "My fellow Introverts, we meet again" — 31,000
  - "I feel like a robot trying to understand how humans behave" — 8,400
  - "After watching this, I realize I don't just dislike small-talk, I dislike conversation altogether." — 6,200
  - "I have mild autism and this channel is so helpful for understanding human behavior. Thank you!" — 5,900
  - "Man I’m cringing at all the opportunities I missed to make great friends and get myself into rooms with great ppl all because I lacked charisma... Just a bland one." — 4,000
- **Gap the comments expose:** Two gaps. (1) Ecological validity — "You give examples of ppl talking on Camera. Thats a whole other world dude"; every exemplar is a professional entertainer on a talk show, and no one is shown how it lands in an office kitchen. (2) The advice raises self-consciousness rather than lowering it: "these videos, while they help many, probably just make some people even more nervous," and "Too complicated, Imma stay home" at 3,100 likes. There is no practice ladder — the video's answer to overwhelm is to buy the sequential course.
- **Usable for a short?** Yes — the "story gap" is the single most portable mechanism (hint the ending, withhold the how), and the Kanye/Kimmel bland-answer clip is an instantly legible before/after.

## ioa6BjuSOt4 — Getting a Girlfriend is NOT an Achievable Goal
- **Channel / subs / views / likes / true comment count:** HealthyGamerGG / 3,410,000 / 1,339,642 / 44,011 / 6,602
- **Category:** DATE (with a strong ANTI streak — it attacks both self-help advice and his own profession)
- **Thesis (1 sentence):** "Work on yourself" does work, but nobody states the timeline — it takes three to seven years — so people quit at year one and conclude they're the exception.
- **Framework:** Aaron Beck's cognitive model, named: "core beliefs" → "adaptations"/"intermediate beliefs" and rules → "automatic thoughts," equated with the yogic "samskaras." Key extension: "core beliefs don't just shape your thoughts they also shape your perceptions." Second model: the double amplifier — your mind filters out positive signal, and "the internet itself amplifies negative information and reduces positive information." Third: the loot box.
- **Cause of failure it names:** Three stacked. (1) Missing duration — "everyone tells you what to do no one tells you how long it'll take and that's what screws people." (2) Perceptual filtering by core beliefs like "i'm no good and no one will ever want me," so successes get discounted. (3) Unprocessed resentment leaking into the interaction: "then that becomes a self-fulfilling prophecy."
- **First action prescribed:** Interrogate your beliefs before your behaviour — "the first thing you've got to understand is like what are your beliefs about this do you believe that you can find someone do you believe that you are lovable." Then set a five-year target so you stop scoring yourself at year two.
- **Time horizon promised:** Explicit and unusually long: "give it at least five years," "we're talking three years four years five years seven years." He then hedges: "i don't actually think it's gonna take five years but here's the magical thing when you set a target... you won't expect anything in two years."
- **Emphasis ranking:** 1. identity (core beliefs / self-concept) · 2. emotion/motivation (resentment, despair, expectation management) · 3. social/relationships (skills, obliviousness, getting out of your own head)
- **Measurement:** Deliberately none for the outcome, and he attacks the absence institutionally — "being in a stable relationship is not something that we measure it's not an outcome that we're trained towards." The only measurement he endorses is on the *provider* side: his coaching program's feedback loop, "we've revised our coach curriculum six times in two years."
- **Prerequisites assumed:** Five to seven years of runway and patience; capacity to keep acting without feedback; access to therapy or a coach or friends to offload resentment onto; internet community. He explicitly lists what you must build in parallel: "work on your social skills get professionally independent get so get financially independent become professionally successful get into shape physically."
- **Contrarian claims:** "\"work on yourself\"... and like why doesn't that seem to work" then reverses it — "yes it works it just takes a really long time" · "it's sort of like honestly it's like opening a loot box that's what dating is" · "sometimes what it requires is obliviousness on your part" · "any amount of resentment that you have about this whole process is a hundred percent justified and 100 needs to be dealt with" · against his own field: "the one wrong answer is yes" (to "can you help me find a girlfriend"), "i lost a little bit of faith in how hands-off the profession is," "oftentimes when people go to therapy looking for guidance what they get is questions" · "you can get clinical improvements without clinical interventions."
- **Best lines (3, verbatim, ≤25 words each):**
  - "everyone tells you what to do no one tells you how long it'll take and that's what screws people"
  - "finding a romantic partner is opening a loot box"
  - "we're not zoning into the raid yet we're just getting ready to zone in"
- **Audience state from comments:** The most acute pain in the batch — men in their 20s to 50s with zero relationship history, suicidality, and years of compliance with advice that produced nothing. They are not asking for motivation; they are asking whether the machine is real. Several report the video pushing them further toward giving up.
- **Top comments worth quoting (5, verbatim + like count):**
  - "\"Work on yourself\" is the equivalent of \"Have you tried turning it on and off?\"" — 9,700
  - "As a guy in his 50s who's trying to reenter dating, one big understated roadblock is the lack of a network." — 4,400
  - "Unironically, these videos push me closer to giving up.\n\nEdit: given up. Not about a girlfriend - just in general." — 1,300
  - "\"One night we just hooked up\"...yeah, listen, that's the part we don't understand. How do you go from \"oh she is cool to, oh we just hooked up and decided to stay exclusive\". That makes no sense at all." — 378
  - "I'm 36 and haven't even been on a date. Working on myself isn't working" — 58
- **Gap the comments expose:** He supplies a duration but no mechanism, and the comments name it precisely. The 378-like comment demands the missing transition ("that's not a explanation for the \"how\" it's just a story"). Others supply the structural variable he never mentions: no network, no venue, no new people — "You will NOT meet someone, ever, without meeting new people." And telling a man with no dates at 36 to wait five more years lands as a sentence, not a plan; the 1,300-like reply is a person disengaging. The video also spends its second half selling coaching, which reads as the answer to a question ("how, concretely?") it declined to answer for free.
- **Usable for a short?** Yes — the strongest anti-hook available: "everyone tells you what to do, no one tells you how long it takes." Pair the college analogy (imagine nobody told you it takes four years) with the loot-box image. The comment "Work on yourself is the equivalent of Have you tried turning it on and off?" is a ready cold open.

# batch-12

## lj5SzG4XHJo — Use Laziness To Your Advantage - The 20 Second Rule
- **Channel / subs / views / likes / true comment count:** Better Than Yesterday / 2,630,000 / 6,823,962 / 247,815 / 5,903
- **Category:** GEN
- **Thesis (1 sentence):** You don't fix behavior with willpower — you cut ~20 seconds of setup off what you want to do and add ~20 seconds to what you don't.
- **Framework:** "The 20 Second Rule" (Shawn Achor, *The Happiness Advantage*) built on "activation energy" and "default behavior"; two moves — "make your desired actions easy to start, while making your unwanted actions difficult."
- **Cause of failure it names:** Not laziness itself but friction — "the more activation energy an activity requires, the less likely you are to start"; intention alone doesn't beat the default. "Simply telling himself that he'll do it, wasn't enough for him to actually do it."
- **First action prescribed:** "examine your surroundings and think about where you could add or reduce 20 seconds of activation energy" — concretely, the guitar-out-of-the-closet / batteries-out-of-the-remote swap; get the gym bag ready ahead of time; throw out the unhealthy food; phone in another room.
- **Time horizon promised:** None stated ("as days passed, his urge to watch TV decreased").
- **Emphasis ranking:** 1. environment 2. habits/systems 3. biology/dopamine (only as "laziness"/convenience-seeking; motivation is explicitly ruled out as unreliable)
- **Measurement:** None. No tracking, no metric, no streak. Only self-observation of which behavior you defaulted to.
- **Prerequisites assumed:** Ownership of your physical space (can rearrange furniture, remove food, pick a gym on your commute), a phone/computer, discretionary money for a gym near work, a Sunday free for meal prep.
- **Contrarian claims:** "Let's be honest. / We're all lazy to some extent. / And there's nothing wrong with that. / It's natural." Also anti-perfectionist about its own model: "The 20 second rule probably won't work all the time, but it will make your behavior more intentional, rather than default." And "Of course, 20 seconds is just a suggestion and not an absolute number."
- **Best lines (3, verbatim, ≤25 words each):**
  - "he made playing the guitar 20 seconds easier, while making watching TV 20 seconds harder."
  - "our phones have become anti-focus devices, as they take our attention from whatever we should be doing."
  - "So make your desired actions easy to start, while making your unwanted actions difficult."
- **Audience state from comments:** Self-aware procrastinators watching from bed, treating the video itself as the procrastination. Heavy irony/meme register; several are students. Almost nobody in crisis — this is the mildly-stuck productivity audience.
- **Top comments worth quoting (5, verbatim + like count):**
  - "90% of people watching this video are laying on their bed." — 37,000
  - "\"Let's be honest: we're all lazy to some extent, and there's nothing wrong with that. It's natural.\"\r\nThank God.\r\n*closes video and continues to do nothing for the next 50 years*" — 27,000
  - "Another strategy I've heard and that has helped me before, is to tell yourself you're going to do less than you really want to do. So like, if you want to go to the gym, instead of telling yourself you're going to go to the gym, which  requires a lot of activation energy, you tell yourself you're just going to put your shoes on." — 16,000
  - "\"The cost of procrastination is the life you could've lived\"\n\nNow go study." — 13,000
  - "i'm so lazy i put this video in the \"watch later\" section" — 3,400
- **Gap the comments expose:** The video optimizes the environment but not the *first ten seconds of the body*. Commenters keep supplying what it lacks: chained micro-commitments ("just put your shoes on"), a permission-to-enjoy timer ("counting 20 secondes… at 20, you must stop"), "one word per day." They also want a mechanism against thinking itself — "DO NOT THINK… The moment you start to worry and in your head 'ifs' and 'buts' appear, you're done." And the top comment quietly reports the failure mode: the reassurance ("nothing wrong with that") licenses the inaction.
- **Usable for a short?** Yes — strongest visual mechanism in the batch: guitar moved out of the closet, batteries pulled out of the remote and walked to another room. Two physical props, one number, instantly demonstrable on camera.

## pe3ndaTKjuM — The 3 Brain Strategies That *Actually* Rewire Your Mind (Neuroscience Explained)
- **Channel / subs / views / likes / true comment count:** Dr. Tracey Marks / 2,410,000 / 1,999,911 / 69,011 / 1,453
- **Category:** SCI
- **Thesis (1 sentence):** Your brain keeps rewiring itself for life, so change is not just possible but under your control — if you feed it novelty, self-observation and challenge.
- **Framework:** "neuroplasticity," split into "structural plasticity" and "functional plasticity"; four areas of impact (adaptability; learning and memory; overcoming negative patterns; recovery and healing); three strategies — (1) "learning new skills," (2) "metacognition and mindfulness" (sub-steps: "self-reflection," "thought journaling," "mindful meditation"), (3) "engage in activities that challenge your brain."
- **Cause of failure it names:** Repetition itself is neutral, so the same machinery that builds good pathways entrenches bad ones — "Neuroplasticity does work both ways, so negative thinking and behaviors also reinforce certain pathways in the brain." Secondary causes are physiological/environmental: age, impoverished environment, "hearing loss, vision loss and social isolation," poor sleep/nutrition/exercise.
- **First action prescribed:** Learn something new — "a musical instrument, picking up a new language, or trying a new hobby." Within metacognition, the first concrete move is self-reflection: ask yourself, "Why did I respond that way?" or, "What assumptions am I making?"
- **Time horizon promised:** None stated.
- **Emphasis ranking:** 1. biology/dopamine (neural pathways, hippocampus/amygdala/prefrontal cortex, sleep-nutrition-exercise) 2. skills 3. habits/systems
- **Measurement:** Only "thought journaling" — "Write down your thoughts and analyze them objectively. Look for patterns or cognitive distortions." No outcome metric. Merch substitutes for a tracking system: a 52-card "essential tools card deck" and a "Growth Mindset 2025 calendar" with "52 weeks of exercises or reminders."
- **Prerequisites assumed:** Baseline health and intact senses, money for classes/instruments/decks/calendars, quality sleep and a "balanced diet with minimal processed foods," time for meditation and journaling, access to a "therapist or physician" (implied by the channel), non-isolation.
- **Contrarian claims:** Against the fixed-brain orthodoxy: "For decades, scientists believe that the brain's structure was fixed after childhood." And she declines to oversell her own third strategy: "Even though the clinical effectiveness of brain-training games is debated, any activity that engages your mind can contribute to cognitive flexibility." Also "you can in fact, teach an old dog new tricks."
- **Best lines (3, verbatim, ≤25 words each):**
  - "The exciting part is you're not stuck with those pathways."
  - "Imagine you're walking through a forest and you take the same path every day. Over time, that path becomes more defined and easier to follow."
  - "Every thought, action, and experience has the potential to rewire your brain"
- **Audience state from comments:** Notably older and more medical than the rest of the batch — stroke/cardiac-arrest/coma survivors, TBI, MS, PTSD veterans, a 71-year-old starting a PhD, a 56-year-old who did a master's. They arrive wanting permission to believe recovery is still available. A second cluster is young and stuck (25, Afghanistan, "wasted my best years"). The thread is also visibly infested with astroturfed book-plug comments ("The Unseen Law by Kael Noven," "Manifest and Receive by Eva Hartley").
- **Top comments worth quoting (5, verbatim + like count):**
  - "After an accident, the doctor told me I might never fully recover my memory. But I used visualization techniques and relearned each small memory. After two years, my memory is even better than before!" — 1,300
  - "I'm 71 years old, and I'm constantly trying to find new ways to stimulate my brain. I'm also planning to go back to college in January to get my PH.D. \nI appreciate all you do! ❤❤❤" — 1,000
  - "I appreciate your content. I have been doing well recovering from cardiac arrest which put me in a coma for a month. I was brain dead - when i woke up I couldn't speak, walk, or talk. I couldn't recognize my mother, sister, brothers or children. I am making new memories and strive to get 1% better each day!" — 602
  - "I stopped smoking in 2008, ( I had smoked fir 15 years)\nI never thought my mind would be able to not think about wanting  a cigarette as soon as I wake up. Today it is like I never even smoked and I am so happy about it." — 542
  - "I think the best form of challenging mental activity is to try and learn a completely new skill. Pick up guitar, learn how to dance, try juggling or yoyo; stuff that requires you to move in new ways and will inevitably require lots of failure before success" — 175
- **Gap the comments expose:** The video explains the *substrate* and never the *schedule* — no dosage, no frequency, no duration, no way to know it's working. So commenters write their own protocols in the replies (two numbered recaps of the three strategies, "1% better each day," "requires you to move in new ways and will inevitably require lots of failure"). The recovery stories carry a specificity the video refuses to give: "two years," "24 weeks," "since 2008." Nobody asks what neuroplasticity is; they ask how long and how often.
- **Usable for a short?** Yes for the visual only — the forest-path metaphor (worn trail vs. new trail through undergrowth) is a clean 5-second animation. The content itself has no mechanism to steal; the hook would have to be borrowed from the comments' recovery stories.

## 8krLpRe8jko — The First Things Women Notice About Men
- **Channel / subs / views / likes / true comment count:** Courtney Ryan / 888,000 / 1,664,355 / 28,660 / 5,022
- **Category:** DATE
- **Thesis (1 sentence):** Almost everything women notice first about a man is a maintenance decision, not a genetic one — grooming, dress, scent, posture and smile move you further than bone structure.
- **Framework:** None — a panel survey. Six women rate 15 attributes in fixed order: arms · abs · feet · shoulders · legs · voice · jawline · smile · hair · style/outfit · hands · eyes · scent · posture, then each names her #1. The implicit model is the host's closing frame: "not necessarily what they were born with or things they can change but more so what they do with it."
- **Cause of failure it names:** Nothing about change or failure to change. Its only implicit cause is neglect — dirty nails, unbrushed teeth, "old raggedy clothes," slouching, smelling bad. Men fail by not maintaining, not by being ugly.
- **First action prescribed:** No sequenced instruction. The highest-frequency directives, in transcript order: "brush your teeth," "clip your nails wash your hands," "get a haircut if you need one trim up your facial hair," own "8 to 10 like really nice pieces," wear a light cologne, "have good posture."
- **Time horizon promised:** None stated.
- **Emphasis ranking:** 1. skills (grooming/dress as learnable execution) 2. identity (what your presentation "signals" — discipline, confidence, self-care) 3. social/relationships
- **Measurement:** None — but it supplies a crude scale twice: "you can go from being like a five to a 10 by just having straight teeth" and "you can also go from a five to a 10 with just a haircut."
- **Prerequisites assumed:** Money (Invisalign, whitening, Prada cologne, a wardrobe of 8–10 good pieces, pedicures, a gym habit), a body without disability, and enough social access to be in rooms with these women at all.
- **Contrarian claims:** Against the "women don't care about looks" line, panelists repeatedly refuse the shallow framing while confirming the standard: "definitely abs and again it's not even a shallow thing I'm really into fitness and I work really hard". Against gym-culture maximalism: "you don't have to be huge and bulky don't think that". Against fixed-trait blackpilling: "I feel like a lot of it's genetic so I mean it's fine I don't really judge a guy off a jawline". And the sleeper claim that grooming beats physique — arms/abs/shoulders/butt are repeatedly waved off while teeth, hair, scent and posture get "top of my list."
- **Best lines (3, verbatim, ≤25 words each):**
  - "you can go from being like a five to a 10 by just having straight teeth"
  - "it's more it's also about the discipline of doing it too just you're a disciplined person if you are consistent and you work out"
  - "if you have bad posture you just seem like like lazy unprofessional not confident like why why are you slouching"
- **Audience state from comments:** Men who don't trust the format. The top comment is a methodological objection, not a thank-you. The room is skeptical, faintly resentful, and hunting for contradictions between what the panel says and what it does — they clocked the "I don't care" girl caring about immutable traits, and clocked a panelist slouching while calling good posture easy. Also heavily astroturfed with dating-book plugs ("The Confident Lover," "the bedroom alpha").
- **Top comments worth quoting (5, verbatim + like count):**
  - "This is entertaining and all... but there's often a HUGE difference between what people say stands out (what they are attracted to) and what they are ACTUALLY attracted to." — 5,100
  - "I'd like to see men 's responses to similar questions about women" — 2,700
  - "Courtney: \"..not necessarily what they were born with or things they can't change, but what they do with it.\" Blonde woman: \"Absolutely!\" Same woman in the other video: if he's a 10 but he's short, he becomes a 1. Irony meter just broke" — 1,400
  - "Be careful of the girls that just say \"I don't care\" when it comes to preference because there is always a threshold to what they would prefer having." — 977
  - "You dont ask a fish how to catch a fish. You ask a fisherman how to catch a fish." — 923
- **Gap the comments expose:** Stated preference vs. revealed preference — the single loudest complaint, and the video has no answer to it. Commenters also want the two things the panel was never asked: height and face ("the face is the make or break item"), and they want the list turned into an ordered action plan rather than 15 unweighted opinions. Nobody leaves with a next step; they leave with an argument.
- **Usable for a short?** Partially — the stealable mechanism is the "five to a 10" jump attached to a cheap, controllable fix (teeth, haircut, scent, posture), and the ranked-reveal format itself. Weak for a *change-your-life* short: no arc, no mechanism, and the audience distrusts the source.

## sSo2b4y5nnA — How To Keep Living When You'd Rather Not
- **Channel / subs / views / likes / true comment count:** Sisyphus 55 / 1,310,000 / 1,769,946 / 124,956 / 9,581
- **Category:** MEN
- **Thesis (1 sentence):** Suicidal thinking is a narrowing of consciousness that falsely presents itself as a completed analysis, so the task is to survive the night, then rebuild autonomy, relatedness and competence.
- **Framework:** Two explicit phases. Part one, survive the night: list alternative courses of action; reach out to a person; account for impulsivity and alcohol; remove means; schedule a professional. Then "it's important to phase into part two with a focus on protection" — name what feels unbearable, then the three self-determination needs, "Autonomy," "Relatedness," "competence," plus treating underlying illness and considering medication.
- **Cause of failure it names:** Not moral failure — mechanism. "a primary predictor is low social support and of equal concern an aversion to self-disclosure." Plus impulsivity and alcohol ("drinking alone is present in the overwhelming majority of such deaths"), and narrowed consciousness: "there was no room for concern about significant others, reaching a point of no return." Underneath it, "an incapacity to be ourselves or said differently to lead a life worth living."
- **First action prescribed:** "Hold on for a day at least. Just think it through a bit more. Jot down some alternative things that could also resolve your life circumstances." Immediately followed by: "So, please send that text or email. Please just reach out."
- **Time horizon promised:** One day — "Hold on for a day at least" / "survive the night." Nothing beyond that is promised.
- **Emphasis ranking:** 1. social/relationships 2. emotion/motivation 3. biology/dopamine (impulsivity, alcohol, medication, "biological origins of suicidality")
- **Measurement:** None numeric. The only self-audit tools are written: "Write down your idea of the good life. What does it look like? How close is it to your current life? And what's in the way?" and treating a lapse via naming.
- **Prerequisites assumed:** Any living person to text; ability to pay for care is flagged as conditional ("If you can afford care, make it a priority. Look into community- based resources"); enough executive function to write a list; access to a therapist or physician for the medication conversation. Notably it does *not* assume money, health, friends or stability — it explicitly hedges each.
- **Contrarian claims:** Refuses the standard prevention scripts. On selfishness: "It isn't selfish. Who am I to say that one's pain is bearable and somehow surmountable? I simply don't know. None of us do." It concedes the option rather than forbidding it: "Yes, suicide is certainly one answer, but are there resources for chronic pain management?" On alcohol, against recovery moralism: "Recognize here that drinking wasn't a sinful, evil, or malicious act. And you aren't a bad person. Alcohol served a purpose." And it declines to supply meaning: "I do not know what your why is. Nobody does, but you."
- **Best lines (3, verbatim, ≤25 words each):**
  - "Existence is a burning house and you can either burn to death or jump out of the window."
  - "People can be damaged, but they can't be broken."
  - "Suffering is an ancient teacher. Not something to desire, but something to face with quiet strength when it inevitably comes knocking."
- **Audience state from comments:** The most acute audience in the batch by a wide margin, and the language is uniform: exhaustion rather than sadness. "I am not even depressed anymore dawg, just tired." Not wanting death but wanting out of the loop; grief; feeling trapped; several explicitly reading the comment section for company. Gallows humor is the entry ritual ("algorithm trynna keep me around" at 30k likes).
- **Top comments worth quoting (5, verbatim + like count):**
  - "algorithm trynna keep me around" — 30,000
  - "I don’t want to die, I just don’t want to keep doing this version of living. Everything feels transactional and hollow. I see how it all works, and none of it feels worth the effort. It’s not really sadness, just a kind of disinterest in continuing the loop. The system runs, I run inside it, and nothing changes. Opting out just seems… cleaner." — 12,000
  - "My dad always said hold on another day because you can always quit tomorrow. But once you quit that’s it no going back. He was referring to commercial fishing but I feel like it’s just good life advice." — 5,800
  - "honestly, to me, depression isn't melancholy or sadness. depression feels like stagnation. im not going anywhere. im trapped. i dont want to live like this but every day i wake up and make the same choices. i dont want to kill myself. i just want something more permanent than sleep and less permanent than death. im tired." — 3,800
  - "“Hold on for a day at least”\nis brutal when every day feels more difficult than the last." — 1,800
- **Gap the comments expose:** The video's entire ask is "reach out" — and the comments are full of people for whom that is the broken part, not the fix: "im tired of telling others things i'd like to hear myself," "\"you dont have to suffer alone\" just helps me remember that I am suffering alone." Second gap: it addresses acute crisis, but the dominant reported state is chronic stagnation — "I don't want to die, I just don't want to keep doing this version of living" — for which a one-day hold is not a plan. Nobody asks for meaning; they ask for a different loop.
- **Usable for a short?** Yes, but only the framing, not the topic. Two transferable assets: the burning-house image, and the reframe that a narrowed, certain-feeling mind is a *symptom*, not an analysis — "claiming some omnipotent insight into the future that frankly none of us possess." Requires crisis-safe handling; not a promo vehicle.

## eac-_ys_f_Q — For People Feeling Behind In Life
- **Channel / subs / views / likes / true comment count:** Nathaniel Drew / 1,780,000 / 3,333,059 / 209,652 / 8,898
- **Category:** GEN
- **Thesis (1 sentence):** "Behind" is measured against a stick that isn't yours and a life you can't see, so the feeling is invented — everyone, including the people you envy, has their own version of it.
- **Framework:** None — pure first-person reflection, unscripted, no steps. The nearest thing to a model is the question "whose whose measuring stick? Am I using".
- **Cause of failure it names:** Comparison built on incomplete information, and beneath it a refusal to accept yourself — "that competitiveness and that Need to not be feeling behind right to desperately move forward. I think for me at least I'll just speak in my case is Based out of a lack of self-love". Also the belief that life is schedulable: "It's nonlinear. So you can't calculate where you'll be in five years or ten years or twenty years".
- **First action prescribed:** None prescribed. He models rather than instructs: have the heart-to-heart that reveals the older, more accomplished person is also lost, and then repeat the reminder daily — "I feel like I have to remind myself every day. And I think that's why I wanted to make this to remind myself".
- **Time horizon promised:** None stated. Explicitly rejects horizons ("you can't calculate where you'll be in five years").
- **Emphasis ranking:** 1. identity (self-acceptance) 2. emotion/motivation 3. social/relationships (comparison, the one honest conversation)
- **Measurement:** None — the video is an argument *against* measurement.
- **Prerequisites assumed:** Almost none materially, but socially significant: at least one older, candid peer to have the conversation with, and enough baseline stability to treat the problem as psychological rather than material. He concedes his own position: "I've had a good year. I'm not gonna lie… I have had an exceptional year and it's been full of incredible experiences."
- **Contrarian claims:** Turns the standard virtue into a cost — "I used to think that that was a really good thing because I'm motivated and determined and very forward-thinking but On the same token. I Feel like that's also robbed me of a lot of joy a lot of pleasure". Also indicts himself as the supply side of the problem: "I'm part of the problem if you like who wants to show the worst moments of their lives anyway". And declares the whole feeling fabricated: "That should be a telltale sign that This is like invented".
- **Best lines (3, verbatim, ≤25 words each):**
  - "There's no such thing as being behind in life that's just using a measuring stick that's wrong for you"
  - "It's all living in my brain. Everybody's version of this is different."
  - "What I post Doesn't tell the full story"
- **Audience state from comments:** Late-20s to 30s, watching peers hit visible milestones — marriage, kids, salaries, homes — while unemployed, single, living with parents, or restarting a career. Recurring self-verdict is "failure," and several go past "behind" into "I have no reason to be alive." The comment section functions as a mutual-permission ledger; the most-liked entries are borrowed quotes, not the video's own line.
- **Top comments worth quoting (5, verbatim + like count):**
  - "“It is not uncommon for people to spend their whole life waiting to start living.” \nEckart Tolle" — 17,000
  - "\"Don't compare your behind the scenes with other's highlight reel.\"" — 8,100
  - "I'm 30 years old. Been working in the fashion industry for about 8 years now. I really like my job but I don't wake up excited to go to work, you know? I am now realising that my real passion has always been working with animals. It has been since I was a little girl. I am now working my way through getting a degree in Veterinary Science. It's going to take me 5 years to finish it.  I'll be  35/36 by then. And you know what? I don't mind the age at all. At age 30, I'm going to go be a vet ❤️ I'm not behind, I'm exactly where I need to be to do what I want to do." — 5,500
  - "I'm 28 and I'm feeling this. Almost all my cousins have jobs and got married, while I'm still unemployed without any job experience, still live with my parents and I'm single. All I do at home is helping my elderly mom who's a street food vendor. I feel like a failure as a person. I don't have any achievement in life, I don't have any skill to offer, I don't know what I want in life. I have no reason to be alive." — 2,200
  - "\"Whose measuring stick am I using?\" That's a very good question." — 2,200
- **Gap the comments expose:** He dissolves the feeling and gives nothing to do with the day after. Commenters supply the missing halves themselves: a replacement metric ("I'm not behind, I'm exactly where I need to be"), a behavioral rule ("I try to not do social media that makes me feel behind"), and — most tellingly — one commenter posts a running multi-year update log in a single comment, inventing the progress-tracking the video refuses. A large fraction of the audience is not comparison-anxious but materially stuck (unemployed, ill, bereaved), and reframing the measuring stick does not move an empty CV.
- **Usable for a short?** Yes — "whose measuring stick am I using?" is the cleanest single-line hook in the batch, and the highlight-reel/behind-the-scenes split screen is an obvious visual. Mechanism is thin, so it's a cold-open, not a whole script.

## GjXY-l1EnhQ — Why Bad Habits Stick (and How to Finally Break Free)
- **Channel / subs / views / likes / true comment count:** Dr. Tracey Marks / 2,410,000 / 678,226 / 35,131 / 835
- **Category:** GEN
- **Thesis (1 sentence):** Bad habits survive because they live in the brain's automatic system, get defended by identity statements and cognitive distortions, and are attacked with willpower instead of substitution.
- **Framework:** Three myths debunked ("I just need more willpower" · "if I know it's bad for me, I should be able to stop" · "I just need to eliminate this habit completely"), then "the identity trap," then the distortions ("Just this once" rationalization · "All or nothing thinking" · "false permission giving"), then five strategies: "Environmental design," "implementation intentions," "the replacement rule," "habit stacking," "the 10-minute rule." Closes with "Relapse, isn't failure it's feedback."
- **Cause of failure it names:** Architecture, not character — "willpower lives in your prefrontal cortex… but habits especially the ones that feel automatic are stored in the basil ganglia, your brain's autopilot system so when you try to override a deeply ingrained habit by sheer force, you're asking your conscious brain to fight an automatic system that's faster older and stronger." Compounded by reward-system myopia ("your brain prioritizes immediate rewards over long-term benefits"), self-labels that act as "prescriptions for future behavior," and elimination without substitution leaving "your brain still left craving the reward that it was anticipating."
- **First action prescribed:** The stated action step at the end: "think about one bad habit that you've been trying to change and identify the cognitive distortion that you most often use to justify it. Is it 'just this once' or is it 'I'll start Monday' write it down and then start noticing when it comes up". Earliest tactic given: add friction — "If you want to stop checking your phone, put it in another room."
- **Time horizon promised:** None stated. Explicitly non-linear: "real habit change is rarely a perfect linear process."
- **Emphasis ranking:** 1. habits/systems 2. biology/dopamine (basal ganglia vs. prefrontal cortex, reward system) 3. identity (the identity trap and narrative reframe) — environment is fourth and heavily used but framed as a habit tactic
- **Measurement:** No metrics; a diagnostic loop instead. "treat it like data what triggered it? what thought patterns were active? what can you do differently next time?" Plus writing down your dominant distortion and "start noticing when it comes up." Merch: "Unlock Your Story Digital Workbook."
- **Prerequisites assumed:** Control over your environment (can delete apps, relocate the phone, restock the cabinet), a work/home routine stable enough to stack onto, some literacy/self-observation capacity, and disposable income for the workbook. Notably assumes *no* willpower or motivation — that's the point.
- **Contrarian claims:** "breaking bad habits is not about moral strength". "Willpower is unreliable, some days you have plenty of it and other days it just disappears without any warning." "substitution is a far more effective than elimination." On slips: "Relapse, isn't failure it's feedback." And against pure knowledge-based change: "There's a huge disconnect between rational knowledge and automatic behavior."
- **Best lines (3, verbatim, ≤25 words each):**
  - "you're asking your conscious brain to fight an automatic system that's faster older and stronger"
  - "These identity statements feel like honest descriptions of yourself but they're more than that. They're prescriptions for future behavior."
  - "It's the return, not the mistake that builds resilience."
- **Audience state from comments:** Doomscrollers caught in the act at 2am, drinkers mid-substitution, procrastinators joking about starting tomorrow. Smaller and warmer than the batch average (835 comments on 678k views) — this is a returning, quasi-clinical audience ("my Wednesday appointments with Dr. Tracey"), several of them clinicians. Guilt and shame are named directly, and the relapse reframe is what they thank her for.
- **Top comments worth quoting (5, verbatim + like count):**
  - "Doom scrolled for so long my algorithm became self-aware" — 2,400
  - "I have a habit of procrastinating. After watching this video, I'm breaking that habit tomorrow." — 1,700
  - "Instead of having today be the last day to do something, have it be the first day to do something else" — 1,500
  - "\"I deserve this\" and \"Spoil yourself\" must be some of the most dangerous statements in our time of instant gratification ..." — 196
  - "Instead of buying a beer from the corner store on the way back from work, I went to the supermarket and bought a six-pack of my favourite non-alcoholic beer. Also changing the route from work to home works for me. The next big challenge is to go to the pub and order a soda instead of a pint of beer." — 67
- **Gap the comments expose:** The one thing missing is the on-ramp when mood is already flat — "disregulated dopamine causes the inability to control willpower… there is absolutely no way that I am able to fast or diet or make any breakthrough changes for better wellness" — the model assumes a baseline capacity to execute the plan. Second gap: no duration or milestone, so commenters post their own counters ("24 weeks strong today!", "almost 3 years later, still don't smoke"). Third: the framework is complete enough that the top comment is a faithful 4-point recap — the audience wants a checklist artifact, and the video only gives prose.
- **Usable for a short?** Yes — best *mechanism* in the batch: prefrontal cortex vs. basal ganglia as a losing fistfight ("faster older and stronger"), then the wine-ritual swap (same glass, different chair, different drink) as the fix. Hook, villain, and demo in one, plus a quotable closer.

## yi6gVC61lv0 — You don't like yourself? Create a new version of yourself
- **Channel / subs / views / likes / true comment count:** Thewizardliz / 8,580,000 / 7,938,937 / 468,015 / 8,229
- **Category:** GEN
- **Thesis (1 sentence):** If you don't like yourself, don't accept yourself — invent a higher version of you in concrete detail and route every daily decision through "what would the highest version of myself do."
- **Framework:** No named model, but a repeated decision rule — "what would the highest version of myself do" — plus a de facto sequence: refuse self-acceptance → build an alter ego (Beyoncé/Sasha Fierce, David Goggins as precedents) → specify her down to clothes, dating, hangouts, time use, career, "even down to the haircut" → act as her daily → find your passion via "what would people even pay like ten dollars for" → talk to God / "the law of assumption" and ask specifically → let go of control → mirror affirmations → accept recovery "is not linear" → fall in love with the process → keep goals private because of the "evil eye."
- **Cause of failure it names:** Self-acceptance advice itself, waiting to be saved, talking instead of doing, and broadcasting goals. "nobody's coming to save you nobody will save you not your mom not your not your spouse not your children nobody will." And: "a lot of reason why you guys are not succeeding is because you talk too much and you do less." Also: "if you tell big dreams to small-minded people they will never support you because they do not understand they haven't done it themselves."
- **First action prescribed:** Define the character — "how does the higher version of yourself look like what is she wearing… who do they date where they hang out how do they spend their time what's their career even down to the haircut". Then act on it that morning: get out of bed, gratitude line, positive affirmations, gym shoes on, go. Her own first-ever step was smaller: "I started going for walks every single day I would make sure I would get my ten thousand steps in".
- **Time horizon promised:** None stated. She anchors on her own timeline instead — two years of severe depression, then "I was doing this for a year for free."
- **Emphasis ranking:** 1. identity (the alter ego is the whole product) 2. emotion/motivation 3. social/relationships (family disbelief, privacy, evil eye) — with a strong fourth of faith/manifestation that the given taxonomy has no slot for
- **Measurement:** Almost none, and deliberately so — the only proxies are external proof to other people ("show me what you've done show me what you're doing"), her mother's changed opinion, "seeing every single day that scale go down," and "ten thousand steps." No journaling, no tracker, no review.
- **Prerequisites assumed:** A God or universe you can petition; a body able to walk and lift; a place to live (she names moving back in with mom as the *failure* option); a phone and internet; enough runway to work "for a year for free"; and tolerance for public judgment. Notably assumes *no* money and *no* baseline mental health — she starts from two years in a bedroom.
- **Contrarian claims:** Opens by attacking the mainstream directly: "you should just stay the same do whatever you're doing and don't change because the world needs to accept you as you are and that's it is something i will never ever tell you why because that's the worst advice ever". On self-compassion: "always be kind to yourself in my opinion that's toxic you cannot always be kind to yourself sometimes you need a reality check." Against accountability-partner/goal-sharing orthodoxy: "do not tell me what you want or what you want to accomplish what your ambitions are I could not care less." And she endorses the evil eye as a causal mechanism: "every single time I told somebody something a goal of mine or anything before it actually happened it did not work."
- **Best lines (3, verbatim, ≤25 words each):**
  - "you want your life to change you have to change"
  - "if you start acting like it you'll become it there is no other way"
  - "i went to the mirror i looked at myself and i was like liz you're pathetic"
- **Audience state from comments:** Very young and very raw — 14, 16, 17-year-olds describing rotting in bed, no friends, self-hatred, suicidal ideation. Strong Muslim contingent responding to the prayer section as recognition rather than instruction. They treat her as a friend and a savior ("U literally saved my life"), and the tone is devotional, not analytical. Also astroturfed with manifestation-book plugs.
- **Top comments worth quoting (5, verbatim + like count):**
  - "I really want to change, I NEED TO CHANGE, I really REALLY hate myself and want to get better, thanks for the motivation!" — 19,000
  - "You also have to ask yourself from time to time \"Is the highest version of myself really MY ideal or does it come from other people (e.g. instagram, the \"that girl\"-trend etc.)?\"" — 10,000
  - "im 14 and i sit at home everyday. im slowly rotting on my bed and i dont have friends and im very insecure, but that was the old me. Thanks to Liz my new version will have friends, radiate confidence and will live my teenage dream." — 8,500
  - "As a muslim, that part of '5 times prayer' really hits me. Its not just a ritual, it has huge meaning of talking to God. Liz, cant thank you enough 🤍" — 2,700
  - "And what about the toxic parents when they stop you, judge you, scold you, say bad about you always want to be in control and you just want to be free,  change but you can't progress because these kind of things are always stopping you." — 110
- **Gap the comments expose:** Three holes. (1) Provenance of the ideal — the #2 comment (10k) asks the question the video never does: is the "highest version" yours or Instagram's. (2) No structure, so the top-liked practical comment is a viewer-written 9-step numbered protocol extracted from her monologue — the audience had to build the framework themselves. (3) Agency assumed: minors and people under controlling parents ask what to do when the environment, not the self-concept, is the constraint — and "nobody's coming to save you" is not an answer for a 14-year-old. Her privacy/evil-eye rule also directly blocks the social support the rest of the batch prescribes.
- **Usable for a short?** Strongest in the batch. Three stealable assets: the one-line decision rule ("what would the highest version of myself do") applied live to a dessert / the gym / a meeting; the mirror scene ("liz you're pathetic") as a cold open; and the anti-self-acceptance hook that picks a fight with every other video in this category. Highest engagement numbers here too (468k likes on 7.9M views).
